=== PROMPT VARIANT ===

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
You are going to create N sequences of length 3, satisfying the following conditions.

- For each of k = 1,2,3, the following holds:
- Among the k-th elements of the sequences, each integer from 1 through N appears exactly once.



For this sequence of sequences, define sequences a=(a_1,a_2,\ldots,a_N) and b=(b_1,b_2,\ldots,b_N) as follows.

- Let s_i be the i-th sequence, and let t_i be the reverse of the i-th sequence. When all of these are sorted in lexicographical order, s_i comes a_i-th, and t_i comes b_i-th.
- Here, if there are identical sequences among the 2N sequences, a and b are not defined.

Therefore, if a and b are defined, each integer from 1 through 2N appears exactly once in the concatenation of a and b.
You are given sequences A and B of length N, where each element of A is an integer between 1 and 2N, and each element of B is either an integer between 1 and 2N or -1.
Also, in the concatenation of A and B, each integer other than -1 appears at most once.
How many pairs of sequences a,b are there such that a and b are defined and the following holds for each integer i from 1 through N?

- a_i = A_i.
- b_i = B_i if B_i \neq -1.

Find the count modulo 998244353.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 A_2 \ldots A_N
B_1 B_2 \ldots B_N

Output

Print the count modulo 998244353.

Constraints


- 2 \leq N \leq 3000
- 1 \leq A_i \leq 2N
- 1 \leq B_i \leq 2N or B_i = -1.
- In the concatenation of A and B, each integer other than -1 appears at most once. That is,
- A_i \neq A_j if i \neq j.
- B_i \neq B_j if i \neq j and B_i,B_j \neq -1.
- A_i \neq B_j.


- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

3
2 3 6
-1 1 -1

Sample Output 1

1

For example, consider creating the following three sequences:

- (1,2,3)
- (2,1,1)
- (3,3,2)

In this case, when sorting s_i and t_i lexicographically, we have:

t_2 = (1,1,2) < s_1 = (1,2,3) < s_2 = (2,1,1) < t_3 = (2,3,3) < t_1 = (3,2,1) < s_3 = (3,3,2)

Thus, (a_1,a_2,a_3,b_1,b_2,b_3) = (2,3,6,5,1,4). Here, a matches the given A, and the second element of b also matches that of B, so this is one pair of sequences a,b satisfying the conditions.
On the other hand, if we create the following three sequences, s_1 and t_1 become identical, so a and b are not defined.

- (1,2,1)
- (2,1,3)
- (3,3,2)

In fact, a=(2,3,6), b=(5,1,4) is the only pair of sequences satisfying the conditions.

Sample Input 2

15
5 16 1 12 30 20 4 13 9 8 24 21 26 28 17
-1 -1 6 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 -1 29 -1 -1 -1

Sample Output 2

758094847

Print the count modulo 998244353.

Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Try to avoid patterns like these:
- spending too long comparing nearby plans before committing to one workable approach;
- returning from code to broad algorithm discussion without a concrete contradiction;
- turning one local doubt into a full restart when the current draft only needs a small patch.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

- both v->u: 1.
- opposite: directed 2-cycle, 0.
So for parallel edges, count 1 if same direction, 0 if opposite. This fits directed cycle? A 2-cycle with opposite orientations is directed cycle length 2; with same orientations not directed cycle? For a simple cycle of length L with acyclic orientation, number of linear extensions? Need formula depending on orientation pattern. Let's derive.

Can this be simplified? For a cycle pattern D (descent set), e(D) is the number of permutations with cyclic descent set exactly D. This is known as "cyclic Eulerian number" for a given descent set. There is a formula:
\[
e(D) = \sum_{T \subseteq D} (-1)^{|D|-|T|} \frac{L!}{\prod_{C \in \text{components of } E\setminus T} |C|!}
\]
where components are paths of edges not in T. This is exactly inclusion-exclusion.

Let's compute B EGF properly with explicit 1/l!. For a block of length l, the total weight on a fixed label set S of size l is:
\[
W(S) = \frac{1}{l!} \sum_{\text{permutations }(v_1,\ldots,v_l)\text{ of }S, v_l \text{ even}} \prod_{t=1}^{l-1} c(v_t).
\]
This sum over permutations with last even and product over all but last. We can compute W(S) combinatorially. Note that \(\prod_{t=1}^{l-1} c(v_t) = \frac{\prod_{v\in S} c(v)}{c(v_l)}\). Since c(v_l) is +1 if odd, -1 if even. But v_l must be even, so c(v_l)=-1. Thus product = - ∏_{v∈S} c(v) for any valid permutation? Wait if last even, c(last)=-1, so product internal = (∏_{all} c(v)) / c(last) = (∏ c) / (-1) = -∏ c. This is independent of which even label is last! Great. Then W(S) = (1/l!) * (number of permutations of S whose last element is even) * (-∏_{v∈S} c(v)).
Number of permutations of S with last element even = (l-1)! * (# even labels in S) = (l-1)! * s, where s = number of even labels in S. Thus
\[
W(S) = \frac{(l-1)! s}{l!} (- \prod_{v\in S} c(v)) = \frac{s}{l} (- \prod_{v\in S} c(v)).
\]
But \(\prod_{v\in S} c(v) = (+1)^{r} (-1)^{s} = (-1)^s\), where r odd count, s even count. So -∏ c = -(-1)^s = (-1)^{s+1}. Thus
\[
W(S) = \frac{s}{l} (-1)^{s+1}.
\]
Wow independent of arrangement except counts! Let's test with S={O,E}: l=2,s=1 -> W=1/2 * (-1)^{2}=1/2. Matches. S={E1,E2}: l=2,s=2 -> W=2/2 * (-1)^3 = -1. Matches earlier total -1. Great.

Thus a block's total weight depends only on the number of even labels s and total length l=r+s:
\[
b_{r,s} = W(S) = \frac{s}{r+s} (-1)^{s+1}.
\]
This is for a single block on a fixed label set with r odd, s even. Nice! This simplifies enormously.

Now, the EGF for a single block:
\[
B(x,y) = \sum_{r,s\ge0, r+s>0} b_{r,s} \frac{x^r}{r!} \frac{y^s}{s!}
= \sum_{r,s} \frac{s}{r+s} (-1)^{s+1} \frac{x^r}{r!} \frac{y^s}{s!}.
\]
We can find closed form. Let's compute.

\[
B(x,y) = \sum_{s\ge1} (-1)^{s+1} \frac{y^s}{s!} \sum_{r\ge0} \frac{s}{r+s} \frac{x^r}{r!}.
\]
Use identity \(\frac{s}{r+s} = s \int_0^1 t^{r+s-1} dt\). Then
\[
B = \sum_{s\ge1} (-1)^{s+1} \frac{y^s}{s!} s \int_0^1 t^{s-1} \sum_{r\ge0} \frac{(x t)^r}{r!} dt
= \int_0^1 \sum_{s\ge1} (-1)^{s+1} \frac{s y^s t^{s-1}}{s!} e^{x t} dt.
\]
\(\frac{s}{s!} = \frac{1}{(s-1)!}\). So sum_{s≥1} (-1)^{s+1} (y t)^{s-1} y / (s-1)!? Let's compute:
s y^s t^{s-1}/s! = y (y t)^{s-1}/(s-1)!.
Sum_{s≥1} (-1)^{s+1} y (y t)^{s-1}/(s-1)! = y ∑_{u=0}∞ (-1)^{u+2?} wait s=u+1, (-1)^{s+1}=(-1)^{u+2}=(-1)^u. So = y ∑_{u≥0} (-1)^u (y t)^u/u! = y e^{-y t}.
Thus
\[
B(x,y) = \int_0^1 y e^{-y t} e^{x t} dt = y \int_0^1 e^{(x-y)t} dt = y \frac{e^{x-y}-1}{x-y}.
\]
So
\[
B(x,y) = y \frac{e^{x-y}-1}{x-y}.
\]
This is the EGF with factorial denominators. Nice. For small: expand e^{x-y}-1 = (x-y)+(x-y)^2/2+...; B = y [1 + (x-y)/2 + ...] = y + xy/2 - y^2/2 + ... Matches b_{1,1}=1/2? EGF coefficient of xy is 1/2 yes. Good.

Maybe we can use the cycle EGF with forced edges by modifying the block EGF. Let's pursue the contraction idea more concretely.

\[

This is exactly a "cycle weight" in the exponential formula. Forced edges mean certain adjacencies must appear in the cycles.

There is a standard way to handle forced adjacencies in permutations: Contract each forced edge into a single vertex with a combined type? But the cycle weight depends on the sequence of types along the cycle, including both endpoints of a forced edge. If we contract an edge u->v, the type sequence around the cycle has ... u, v ...; the edge u->v contributes a transition from type(u) to type(v) to the cyclic sign pattern. The cycle weight e(D) depends on the entire cyclic sequence of types, not just edge-by-edge product. However, e(D) itself can be expressed as a sum over T (marked edges) with path weights that factor over paths. In the marked-edge expansion, the weight factorizes over paths, and paths are sequences of vertices with unmarked edges. This is more amenable to forced edges: forced edges can be unmarked (inside paths) or marked (cuts). We can sum over markings of forced edges and then count path collections with some forced adjacencies.

Let's work with the marked-edge expansion including forced edges.

We have:
\[
\text{Ans} = N! \sum_{f \supseteq F} \sum_{T \subseteq D(f), T \neq \emptyset \text{ per cycle}} (-1)^{|D\setminus T|} \prod_{\text{paths }P} \frac{1}{|P|!}.
\]
Actually sign product over unmarked even edges. This is equivalent to:
\[
\text{Ans} = N! \sum_{(f,T)} w(f,T)
\]
where w = (-1)^{# even unmarked edges} / ∏ path lengths!, and T is a set of marked edges (subset of even-source edges) such that every cycle of f has at least one marked edge. The pair (f,T) is in bijection with:
- A set of directed paths covering all vertices (unmarked edges are the edges within paths).
- A permutation ρ on paths (marked edges connect end of each path to start of next), with the condition that every marked edge source is even (since T⊆D), and every cycle of ρ? Wait every cycle of f corresponds to a cycle of ρ. The condition "every cycle of f has at least one marked edge" is automatically satisfied because each path in a cycle has one marked outgoing edge; a cycle of f with k paths has k marked edges, k≥1. If T empty for a cycle, that would mean no marked edges in that cycle, impossible because a cycle of f must have at least one edge; in path representation, if T empty, the "path" would be the whole cycle with no start/end, not represented. So every cycle of f has at least one marked edge iff the path representation has at least one path per f-cycle, which is true by construction. Good.

Now, forced edges F: For each forced edge u->v, in the path representation, either:
- u->v is unmarked: then u and v are consecutive in the same path.
- u->v is marked: then u is the end of some path, v is the start of some path, and ρ maps that path to the path starting at v.

We can sum over the marking status of each forced edge. Since forced edges are fixed, if a forced edge is marked, its source u must be even (otherwise impossible because T⊆D). If unmarked, source u can be even (with sign -1) or odd (sign +1).

This suggests we can build paths where some adjacencies are forced unmarked, and some path ends/starts are forced by marked edges. The permutation ρ on paths has some forced mappings: if forced edge u->v is marked, then the path ending at u maps to the path starting at v. This is a partial permutation on paths. Counting over ρ with forced mappings is again a permutation problem but on paths; however, the number of paths is variable and the forced mappings among paths may form chains/cycles. This could be handled recursively? Maybe not.

But perhaps we can choose to mark all forced edges? No, we must sum over all T. This is like evaluating the same EGF with some adjacencies pinned.

Let's consider using the cycle weight e(D) directly and the fact that forced edges are just adjacencies in cycles. There is a known method: Sum over permutations with cycle weights that depend on cyclic sequences can be computed by a transfer matrix over the "types" (odd/even) and forced components. Let's see if the EGF path representation can be modified to count derangements by using the sequence-of-paths with a twist: A permutation ρ on paths with no fixed points on singleton paths. The number of such ρ for a given path collection with k paths, among which s are singleton paths, is the number of permutations of k elements with no fixed points on a specified subset of size s (but fixed points on non-singleton paths allowed). This is not simply k!. We can compute by inclusion-exclusion over singleton paths:
\[
\#\{\rho: \rho(P)\ne P \text{ for singleton }P\} = \sum_{j=0}^{s} (-1)^j \binom{s}{j} (k-j)!.
\]
Because force j singleton paths to be fixed, remaining k-j paths arbitrary. Thus for a path collection, the sum over ρ is this number, not k!. In the ordered-list replacement, we need weight depending on s (number of singleton paths). This can be incorporated into the EGF by marking singleton paths.

Let's analyze the graph D_f more. It is a directed graph on vertices with edges i -> f(i) if A_i odd, f(i) -> i if A_i even. This is an orientation of the cycles of f. The number of linear extensions of a disjoint union of oriented cycles. Let's compute e(D) for a cycle using the "cycle lemma" maybe equals the number of "cyclic permutations" of the values? Let's brute derive formula.

We have a cycle of L vertices with signs σ_i (edge i->i+1 if +, i+1->i if -). We want number of assignments of ranks 1..L to vertices satisfying sign constraints. This is the number of permutations π of [L] with cyclic descent set D (where D = positions of - signs). There is a known formula:
\[
e(D) = \sum_{j=0}^{L-1} (-1)^j \binom{L}{j} \#\{\text{...}\}
\]
Let's search memory: The number of permutations with a given cyclic descent set D is
\[
e(D) = \sum_{T \subseteq D} (-1)^{|D|-|T|} \frac{L!}{\prod_{C \in \pi_0(T)} |C|!}
\]
which we have. This can be computed in O(L^2) for a given D. But summing over f cycles with forced edges might be possible via the EGF for cycles where the weight is this inclusion-exclusion. We can interchange sums at the level of cycles: A cycle of f with vertex set S and cyclic order (determined by f) has weight e(D). In the exponential formula for permutations, cycles are unlabeled cyclic sequences. Forced edges F specify some adjacencies in the cycles. We can use the "cycle index" of permutations with specified adjacencies by considering the partial permutation F as a set of "blocks" that must appear as consecutive elements in cycles. There is a standard technique: If a permutation has a set of required adjacencies (a partial permutation), the exponential generating function for such permutations is obtained by "contracting" each component of the required adjacency graph. Let's apply this to the cycle weight e(D) using the inclusion-exclusion path expansion, but perhaps we can contract forced edges in the path expansion and still get a manageable EGF.

Let's analyze forced edges in the path expansion more concretely. A forced edge u->v is an edge of f. In the path representation, if it is unmarked, it is an internal edge of a path. If marked, it is a cut. The set of forced edges F is a partial injection. Consider the graph of forced edges. We can decide for each forced edge whether it is marked. This partitions each forced component into subpaths of consecutive forced edges with the same marking? Actually if a forced edge is unmarked, it connects two vertices inside a path. If marked, it connects end of one path to start of another. In both cases, the order u before v is preserved in the path structure: if unmarked, u immediately precedes v in the same path; if marked, u is the end of some path and v is the start of the next path in the ρ cycle. Thus in the sequence of paths (if we follow ρ), u is immediately followed by v across a path boundary. So in the final permutation f, u->v is always an adjacency in the cyclic order of f. The path representation just decides whether the adjacency is within a path or between paths.

Now, if we contract each forced edge u->v, regardless of marking, the path representation becomes a representation of a permutation on contracted objects. Maybe the marking of forced edges can be integrated into the weight of the contracted object.

Let's define a "super-vertex" for each connected component of the forced graph F. Since F is a partial injection, components are paths or cycles. If a component is a directed cycle, it is already a cycle of f; no unforced edges enter/leave. We can handle separately. If a component is a directed path v_1 -> v_2 -> ... -> v_l, then in f, this path appears as a contiguous block. The incoming edge to v_1 and outgoing edge from v_l are unforced. In the path representation, the forced edges within the block may be marked or unmarked. If a forced edge v_t -> v_{t+1} is marked, then the block is split between v_t and v_{t+1}: v_t is end of a path, v_{t+1} is start of next path (in ρ). If unmarked, they are in the same path.

Thus a forced path component can be seen as a sequence of l vertices with some cuts (marked forced edges) between them. The cuts split the component into "segments" of consecutive vertices connected by unmarked forced edges. Each segment is a subpath in the path representation (possibly extended by unforced edges before/after). The marked forced edges are edges of ρ between segments: the segment ending at v_t maps to the segment starting at v_{t+1}. Therefore, at the level of segments, the forced component imposes that these segments are consecutive in the ρ cycle. If we contract each segment into a "macro-path", then the ρ permutation has some forced adjacencies between macro-paths. This is again a partial permutation on macro-paths.

But maybe we can choose to treat the entire forced path component as a single "macro-vertex" in the permutation f, and the internal cuts only affect the weight, not the external permutation. The external permutation on macro-vertices is a permutation where each macro-vertex has an incoming unforced edge and an outgoing unforced edge (except forced cycles). The weight of a macro-vertex in the marked expansion can be computed by summing over internal markings, yielding a weight that depends only on the macro-vertex's sequence of vertex signs and length. Then the problem reduces to counting permutations of macro-vertices with cycle weights that are products of macro-vertex weights? Wait the path weight 1/|P|! couples the macro-vertex with adjacent unforced edges because paths can span across macro-vertices: an unforced edge from end of one macro-vertex to start of another may be unmarked, merging them into the same path. Thus macro-vertices are not independent; unforced edges can be marked or unmarked too, and paths can cross macro-vertex boundaries. So contraction doesn't fully separate.

- If A_i odd, allowed images are positions > p.

The number of permutations with these restrictions (and forced edges) is the permanent of a 0-1 matrix with a "staircase" shape. There is a known formula: The number of permutations with row restrictions of the form column > row or column < row is the Eulerian number? Let's derive the number of h with excedance set exactly S and some forced pairs (p,q). In the fixed Y, the forced F edges become forced pairs in h: h(p_u)=p_v. The number of h with excedance set S and containing a partial injection H (on positions) is a known quantity: it is the number of permutations with a given excedance set and some prescribed values. This can be computed by inclusion-exclusion over the excedance set:
\[
\#\{h: Ex(h)=S, h(p)=q \forall (p,q)\in H\} = \sum_{T\subseteq S} (-1)^{|S|-|T|} \#\{h: Ex(h)\subseteq T, h(p)=q\}.
\]
The condition Ex(h)⊆T means for rows not in T, h(row) ≤ row; for rows in T, no restriction. With forced pairs, this is a permanent of a Ferrers board with some forced cells. Maybe there is a simpler way: Since N=3000, we can use dynamic programming over the permutation f directly with the transfer-matrix method for cycle weights, and incorporate forced edges by treating them as "broken" cycles. Let's attempt to derive a DP over the partial permutation F components.

The sum over permutations f extending F of ∏ e(C)/|C|! can be computed using the exponential formula for permutations with cycle weights, where some cycles are partially specified. The forced edges F form a partial permutation. We can complete F to a permutation by choosing a permutation on the "gaps". This is analogous to counting permutations with prescribed cycles/edges using the "cycle index" and can be done by considering the components of F.

Let's classify the components of F (partial injection):
- Directed cycles: fully forced cycles. They contribute a fixed factor e(C)/|C|! and are independent.
- Directed paths: chains of forced edges. Let a path component have start s (no incoming forced) and end t (no outgoing forced). In any completion, this path is embedded in a cycle of f. If we contract the path into a single "super-vertex" with a start and end, the cycle of f becomes a cycle of super-vertices connected by unforced edges. The weight of a cycle of super-vertices is the sum over markings of all edges (unforced and forced internal) of the path weight. This is like the original cycle weight but on a sequence of super-vertices, where each super-vertex has an internal structure that affects the path decomposition.

Maybe we can compute the total weight of all cycles of super-vertices using the same EGF F(x,y), but with the "atom" being a super-vertex with a certain weight generating function. Let's try to define a super-vertex for a forced path component as a "block" that must appear contiguously in the cycle. In the path representation, if we contract the entire forced path into a super-vertex, the internal forced edges can be marked or unmarked. The super-vertex as a whole has a start vertex and an end vertex. In the path representation, the super-vertex may be split by internal marked edges into multiple paths, but those paths are connected by forced ρ mappings. However, if we are summing over all ρ, the forced internal marked edges are just part of ρ. This suggests that a forced path component can be represented as a sequence of "atoms" with some forced ρ edges between them. The external unforced edges connect the end of the component to the start of another component.

This is essentially the same problem on a smaller set of components, but with components having internal structure. We could recursively apply the same method, but the components are paths of forced edges; the internal forced edges are already fixed, so the problem reduces to counting permutations of components with weights that can be computed by the same EGF but with "colored" atoms? e(D) = ∑_{T⊆D} (-1)^{|D|-|T|} N(T).
If u->v is in D (source u even), then T may include it or not. If u->v not in D (source u odd), T cannot include it; it is always unmarked. Contracting the edge would remove the vertex v and the edge u->v; the path lengths in N(T) change. This is not a simple type merge.

Let's formalize the path representation as a model on the cycles of f. Each cycle of f is a cyclic sequence of vertices. Each edge (i -> f(i)) has a state: marked (in T) or unmarked. Constraints:

- Every cycle has at least one marked edge.
- Weight: for each unmarked edge from vertex i, factor c(i) (where c(i)=-1 if even, +1 if odd); for each marked edge, factor 1.
- Additionally, each maximal run of unmarked edges (a path) of length l (number of vertices? Let's define path length as number of vertices in the run) contributes 1/l!.

This is a 1D statistical mechanics model on cycles with long-range 1/l! interaction. The 1/l! makes it non-local. But the EGF F(x,y) handled this non-locality via the sequence EGF. Now, count permutations with no monochromatic cycles and forced edges. This is a classic problem that can be solved by inclusion-exclusion over monochromatic cycles or by exponential generating functions. Since colors are only two, and forced edges are partial injection.

Let's formulate.

We have N labeled vertices, each colored odd (O) or even (E). Let R = #O, M = #E. We need count permutations f (bijection) such that:
- f contains a given partial injection F (a set of directed edges, forming paths/cycles).
- No cycle of f is entirely O or entirely E. Equivalently, every cycle has at least one O and one E.

This is equivalent to f being a permutation whose cycles are "bicolored". For no forced edges, the number of permutations with no monochromatic cycles is known: total permutations minus those with some mono cycles. Using inclusion-exclusion over cycles, the EGF is:
\[
\exp\left( \sum_{n\ge1} \frac{x^n}{n} + \sum_{n\ge1} \frac{y^n}{n} - \sum_{n\ge1} \frac{x^n+y^n}{n} \right)?
\]
Wait a permutation is a set of cycles. A cycle can be all O, all E, or mixed. The EGF for cycles of labeled vertices with colors:
- All-O cycles: any cyclic order of O vertices. EGF for all-O cycles: \(\sum_{n\ge1} O^n/n = -\log(1-O)\).
- All-E cycles: \(-\log(1-E)\).
- Mixed cycles: cycles containing at least one of each. The EGF for all cycles (any colors) is \(-\log(1 - O - E)\). Thus mixed cycles EGF = \(-\log(1-O-E) + \log(1-O) + \log(1-E)\)? Wait all cycles EGF includes all-O, all-E, mixed. So mixed = total - allO - allE = \(-\log(1-O-E) + \log(1-O) + \log(1-E)\).
Then permutations with no mono cycles are sets of mixed cycles, EGF:
\[
\exp(\text{mixed cycles EGF}) = \frac{(1-O)(1-E)}{1-O-E}.
\]
Because exp(-log(1-O-E)) = 1/(1-O-E), exp(log(1-O))=1-O, exp(log(1-E))=1-E.
Thus the EGF for permutations with no monochromatic cycles is
\[
G(O,E) = \frac{(1-O)(1-E)}{1-O-E}.
\]
This is for labeled vertices with colors, using exponential generating functions in O,E (ordinary variables for counts, with factorial denominators). Let's test small N.

For N=2, R=1,M=1. Coefficient of O E in G? Expand 1/(1-O-E) = 1 + O+E + O^2+2OE+E^2+...
(1-O)(1-E)=1-O-E+OE.
Product: (1-O-E+OE) * (1 + O+E + O^2+2OE+...)
Coefficient OE: from 1*2OE =2, (-O)*E = -1, (-E)*O = -1, OE*1=1. Total 1. EGF coefficient for O^1 E^1 is 1. Number of structures on fixed labels = R!M! * coeff? Wait G is EGF with coefficients g_{r,s} = count/(r!s!). Coefficient of O E is 1, so count =1!*1!*1=1. Indeed only transposition, mixed cycle, count1. Good.

For N=3, R=1,M=2. Compute coefficient of O^1 E^2 in G. Let's expand. G = (1-O)(1-E)/(1-O-E). We can compute coefficient. Let H=1/(1-O-E)=∑_{n≥0}(O+E)^n. (1-O-E+OE)H = H - (O+E)H + OE H = H - (O+E)H + OE H = (1 - (O+E) + OE)H. But H=1/(1-(O+E)), so (1-(O+E))H=1. Thus G = 1 + OE H. Wow! Because (1-O)(1-E) = 1 - (O+E) + OE, and H=1/(1-(O+E)), so G = 1 + OE/(1-O-E). Nice.

So G(O,E) = 1 + \frac{O E}{1 - O - E}.
This is much simpler. Coefficient for R=1,M=2: OE/(1-O-E) = OE ∑_{n≥0}(O+E)^n. Coefficient O^1 E^2: from n=2? (O+E)^2 has OE coefficient2; times OE gives O^2 E^2? Wait OE * (O+E)^n. Need total O^1 E^2. n=1: OE*(O+E) gives O^2E + OE^2. OE^2 coefficient 1. So coefficient =1. Count = R!M! * coeff =1!*2!*1=2. Matches no-partial feasible b count (2 derangements). Good.

So the EGF for no mono cycles is extremely simple:
\[
G(O,E) = 1 + \frac{O E}{1 - O - E}.
\]
This counts permutations with no monochromatic cycles, no forced edges. The number for R odd, M even is:
If R=0 or M=0, G=1, count=0 (unless N=0). For R,M>0, coefficient of O^R E^M in OE/(1-O-E) = coefficient of O^{R-1} E^{M-1} in 1/(1-O-E) = binom(R+M-2, R-1). Then count = R! M! * binom(N-2, R-1). Let's test N=3,R=1,M=2: 1!*2!*binom(2,0)=2. N=2,R=1,M=1:1*1*binom(0,0)=1. N=4,R=2,M=2: 2!*2!*binom(2,1)=8. Let's maybe.

Now, we need incorporate forced edges F. This is a permutation with some specified edges and no monochromatic cycles. This can be handled by the same EGF with forced edges using inclusion-exclusion or contraction. Since the no-mono condition is just "cycles are mixed", and forced edges are partial injection, we can use the exponential formula with forced edges.

A partial injection F consists of directed edges. In any permutation f containing F, the cycles of f are formed by completing the components of F. We can count permutations with no mono cycles and forced edges by considering the components of F.

Let's analyze F components:
- Directed cycle component: This is already a cycle of f. It is valid iff it contains at least one O and one E. If valid, it contributes a factor 1 (the cycle is fixed) and uses those vertices. If invalid, answer 0.
- Directed path component: A chain of forced edges v1 -> v2 -> ... -> vl. In f, this path is part of a larger cycle. We can contract this path into a single "super-vertex" with a color? The cycle containing it will include the sequence of vertices v1,...,vl consecutively. For the cycle to be mixed, it suffices that the whole cycle has at least one O and one E. If the forced path itself already contains both colors, then any cycle containing it is automatically mixed. If the forced path is monochromatic (all O or all E), then the rest of the cycle must contain at least one vertex of the opposite color.

This suggests we can contract each forced path component into a super-vertex with a "color type":
- If the path contains both colors, it's a "mixed" super-vertex. Any cycle containing it is valid regardless of other vertices.
- If the path is all O, it's an "O-type" super-vertex. A cycle containing it must also contain at least one E somewhere (either another super-vertex or free vertex).
- If all E, similarly.

But wait, a forced path component may have length >1 and contain both colors; contracting it to a single mixed super-vertex is valid because the internal colors ensure the cycle is mixed. The external cycle just needs to be a permutation of super-vertices with no monochromatic cycles? If a super-vertex is mixed, then any cycle containing it is automatically mixed, so the no-mono condition on the external permutation is satisfied for that cycle regardless of other super-vertices. In terms of counting permutations of super-vertices with no mono cycles, a mixed super-vertex acts as a "wildcard" that can be in any cycle and makes it valid. This complicates the simple two-color EGF, but maybe we can handle by inclusion-exclusion.

Alternatively, use inclusion-exclusion over monochromatic cycles in the original permutation with forced edges. The EGF for no mono cycles with forced edges can be derived using the "cycle index" and the partial injection F.

Let's use the exponential formula with forced edges. The number of permutations with no mono cycles and containing a partial injection F can be obtained by the principle of inclusion-exclusion over the forced edges? Or by the "exponential formula with a specified set of edges" using the fact that the EGF for permutations with no mono cycles is \(1 + OE/(1-O-E)\). Forced edges can be handled by differentiating the EGF: The number of permutations with a set of specified edges is the coefficient of the product of the corresponding "pointing" operators. There is a known result: If a permutation class has EGF G(x), the number of permutations containing a given set of directed edges (a partial injection) is the coefficient of the derivative of G with respect to those edges? More concretely, in the exponential formula, forcing an edge u->v in a permutation corresponds to marking that u is immediately followed by v in a cycle. In the EGF, this is equivalent to taking a derivative with respect to the "cycle" variable and multiplying by something. For the class of permutations with no mono cycles, the EGF is \(G(O,E)\). If we force a set of edges F that is a partial injection, we can contract each forced edge in the EGF? Let's derive.

Consider the class of permutations as sets of cycles. The EGF for all permutations is \(\exp(\sum_{n≥1} (O^n+E^n+(O+E)^n? )/n)\). Forcing an edge u->v means in the cycle, u is followed by v. In the labelled EGF, the number of cycles containing a specified directed edge can be computed by "rooting" the cycle at that edge. If we contract the edge u->v into a single vertex w, the cycle length decreases by1, and the colors combine? For the no-mono condition, the contracted vertex has a color that is the union of colors of u and v. If u and v have different colors, the contracted vertex is "mixed"; if same, it has that color. Then a cycle in the contracted permutation is mixed iff the original cycle is mixed? Let's check: Original cycle has a set of colors. Contracting u,v removes one vertex but the set of colors in the cycle remains the same (union of colors of all vertices). If u,v have different colors, the contracted vertex is mixed, but the cycle's color set is still the union of all original colors. The no-mono condition depends on the cycle's color set, not on individual vertices. If we contract a mixed pair into a single mixed vertex, the cycle's color set remains the same (it still contains both colors because the mixed vertex represents both). If we contract a same-color pair, the cycle's color set remains the same (the contracted vertex has that color, and other vertices unchanged). So the no-mono condition is preserved: a cycle is mixed iff the contracted cycle is mixed, provided we treat a mixed contracted vertex as containing both colors. However, the standard two-color EGF assumes each vertex is either O or E, not mixed. We can introduce a third type "M" (mixed) for contracted vertices that contain both colors. A cycle is valid if it contains at least one O and one E, but an M vertex counts as both. In terms of color set, a cycle with an M vertex is automatically valid. So we need count permutations of colored vertices (O/E/M) with no mono cycles, where M vertices are "both".

Forced edges F may form paths. Contracting each maximal forced path component into a super-vertex:
- If the path contains both O and E, type M.
- If all O, type O.
- If all E, type E.
- If the component is a directed cycle, it's a fixed cycle; check if its color set is mixed (or if it contains M? no M before contraction). If not, answer 0; if yes, it contributes a fixed valid cycle and we can remove it.

After contracting all forced path components, we have K super-vertices, each of type O, E, or M. We need count permutations of these K super-vertices (with no additional forced edges) such that every cycle is valid: a cycle is valid if it contains at least one O and one E, or contains at least one M (since M has both). Wait if a cycle contains an M and no other vertices, is it valid? An M super-vertex represents a forced path with both colors, so the original cycle contains both colors, yes valid. Thus any cycle containing M is valid. Cycles without M must contain at least one O and one E.

This is a generalization of the two-color problem with a third color M that is "universal". We need count permutations of K labeled items with types O,E,M such that every cycle either contains an M or contains both O and E. Let's call this "valid cycles".

Can we find the EGF for this? Let variables O,E,M. A cycle is:
- Invalid if it contains no M and only O (all-O cycle).
- Invalid if no M and only E (all-E cycle).
- Valid otherwise: contains M, or contains both O and E.

All cycles EGF (any colors) is \(-\log(1 - O - E - M)\).
Invalid cycles:
- All-O cycles: \(-\log(1-O)\).
- All-E cycles: \(-\log(1-E)\).
No other invalid cycles (a cycle with M is valid; a cycle with O and E no M valid).
Thus valid cycles EGF = \(-\log(1-O-E-M) + \log(1-O) + \log(1-E)\).
Permutations with only valid cycles EGF:
\[
G(O,E,M) = \exp(\text{valid cycles}) = \frac{(1-O)(1-E)}{1-O-E-M}.
\]
This is a simple generalization! For M=0, reduces to previous.

Now, the number of permutations of K super-vertices with no invalid cycles is:
\[
K! \cdot [O^{o} E^{e} M^{m}] G(O,E,M)
\]
where o,e,m are counts of super-vertex types, with factorial denominators? Wait G is EGF with variables O,E,M and coefficients count/(o!e!m!). The total number on a fixed set of super-vertices is o! e! m! * coefficient. Since all super-vertices are labeled, yes.

We can simplify G:
\[
G = \frac{(1-O)(1-E)}{1-O-E-M}.
\]
Let S = O+E+M. Then denominator 1-S. We can expand.

We need coefficient for given o,e,m. Let's derive formula.

\[
G = (1-O)(1-E) \sum_{n≥0} (O+E+M)^n.
\]
Let N = o+e+m = K. We need coefficient of O^o E^e M^m.

Since (1-O)(1-E) = 1 - O - E + OE.

Coefficient = coeff in H = 1/(1-O-E-M) minus coeff of O*H minus coeff of E*H plus coeff of OE*H.

Let H coefficient h(a,b,c) = multinomial (a+b+c)!/(a!b!c!) for O^a E^b M^c (since (O+E+M)^n coefficient n!/(a!b!c!), and sum over n=a+b+c gives (a+b+c)!/(a!b!c!)).

Then:
Coeff G(o,e,m) = h(o,e,m) - h(o-1,e,m) - h(o,e-1,m) + h(o-1,e-1,m),
with h=0 if any index negative.

This is the EGF coefficient (with factorial denominators). The actual count of permutations on the labeled super-vertices is:
\[
\text{Count} = o! e! m! \cdot \text{Coeff}.
\]
Let's simplify. h(a,b,c) = (a+b+c)!/(a!b!c!). Then:
o!e!m! * h(o,e,m) = K!.
o!e!m! * h(o-1,e,m) = o!e!m! * (K-1)!/((o-1)!e!m!) = o * (K-1)!.
Similarly second = e*(K-1)!.
Third = o!e!m! * (K-2)!/((o-1)!(e-1)!m!) = o e * (K-2)!.
Thus Count = K! - o (K-1)! - e (K-1)! + o e (K-2)!.
Factor (K-2)!:
Count = (K-2)! [ K(K-1) - (o+e)(K-1) + o e ].
Since o+e = K - m. Let's express:
K(K-1) - (K-m)(K-1) + o e = (K-1)m + o e.
So
\[
\text{Count} = (K-2)! \left( m (K-1) + o e \right)
\]
for K≥2. What about K=1? If K=1, a single super-vertex. Valid iff it is M (mixed) because a cycle with one M is valid; if O or E invalid. Formula with (K-2)! invalid. Need handle K=1 separately: Count = 1 if m=1 and o=e=0, else 0. For K=0, count 1 (empty permutation) if no forced cycles? But N≥2, after removing forced cycles maybe K could be 0; then count 1 if all vertices in valid forced cycles.

Let's test with no forced edges: K=N, super-vertices are original vertices, m=0, o=R, e=M. Formula for N≥2: Count = (N-2)! (0*(N-1)+R*M) = (N-2)! R M. But earlier we had count = R! M! * binom(N-2,R-1). Are these equal? Let's check N=3,R=1,M=2: formula (1)!*2=2. R!M!*binom(1? wait binom(N-2,R-1)=binom(1,0)=1) *2=2. For N=4,R=2,M=2: formula (2)!*4=8. R!M!*binom(2,1)=2*2*2=8. Are they equal generally? R!M! * (N-2)!/((R-1)!(M-1)!) = R*M*(N-2)! because R!/(R-1)!=R, M!/(M-1)!=M. Yes! So formula matches. Great.

Now, what about forced path components that are monochromatic and have length >1? Contracting them to a single O or E super-vertex is valid because the cycle's color set is unchanged. The number of super-vertices K = N - (number of forced edges) + (number of path components)? Let's compute: Each forced edge reduces vertex count by1 when contracting a path. A forced path component with l vertices and l-1 forced edges contracts to 1 super-vertex, reducing count by l-1 = number of edges in component. Forced cycle component with l vertices and l edges is removed entirely (not a super-vertex), reducing count by l. So K = N - (total forced edges in path components) - (total vertices in forced cycles)? More simply, K = number of path components + number of free vertices (vertices not touched by F). Because each path component becomes one super-vertex; each free vertex becomes one super-vertex. Forced cycles are removed. Yes.

The types of super-vertices:
- Free vertex: type O if its A parity odd, E if even.
- Forced path component: type M if it contains both parities, O if all vertices odd, E if all even.
- Forced cycle component: must contain both parities (or if it contains? A forced cycle is a full cycle of f; it is valid iff it contains at least one O and one E. Since no M. If valid, it contributes no super-vertex and no choice; if invalid, answer 0. Also if a forced cycle has length1 fixed point, it's all one color, invalid unless? Fixed point is invalid because cycle mono. So answer 0 if any forced cycle is monochromatic. Note: A forced cycle of length2 with one O one E is valid. Good.)

Then the number of permutations of super-vertices with valid cycles is given by the formula above. But wait, the super-vertices are labeled by their original vertex sets? They are distinguishable. The count formula using EGF with labels treats each super-vertex as a distinct labeled item. Since forced path components are distinct, yes.

Is there any additional constraint from the original distinctness of sequences that we already encoded as no monochromatic cycles? Yes, no fixed points and no mono cycles. A cycle with both colors is acyclic, so valid. What about a cycle with an M super-vertex representing a forced path with both colors: the original cycle is mixed, so valid. Good.

Let's test sample 1 with this method.

N=3, colors: 1 E, 2 O, 3 E. F={1->2} (from specified B). F component: path 1->2 length2, contains E and O -> type M. Free vertex: 3 type E. So K=2 super-vertices: one M, one E. o=0,e=1,m=1. Formula for K=2: Count = (0)!? (K-2)! =0! =1, m(K-1)+oe =1*1+0=1. Count=1. Matches sample.

If no F: K=3, o=1,e=2,m=0. Count=(1)!*(0+2)=2. Matches.

Great! This seems the intended solution.

Now we need handle the partial F construction from input and validate A/B.

Let's outline steps:

2. Validate A:

If B_i not in A and v=ceil(B_i/2), then B_i is the other. If A somehow contains neither? A valid ensures contains one. So okay.

The no-mono cycle condition will reject fixed points because a fixed point is a monochromatic cycle (color of vertex). But wait if the vertex is? A fixed point cycle length1 is mono, invalid.      - indegree array.

   - Since N=3000, simple O(N) per component okay.

   Let's design robust component detection:

   - indeg array.

Mark those as cycle. If we hit a previously visited vertex, the current path attaches to an already processed component; mark current path as path (if it didn't enter a cycle? Now, one subtlety: The partial injection F may have a path component that is a single vertex? A free vertex is not in F; we treat as super-vertex of its color. A forced path component has at least one edge, so length≥2. But what about a vertex that is in the domain but not image? That's a path start; it's part of a path component. Good.

Now, let's verify the EGF formula for super-vertices with M type more rigorously.

We have K labeled super-vertices, each with type O, E, or M. We need count permutations where every cycle is valid: a cycle is valid if it contains at least one M, or at least one O and one E. The EGF for valid cycles:
All cycles over types O,E,M: \(-\log(1 - O - E - M)\).
Invalid cycles: all-O cycles \(-\log(1-O)\), all-E cycles \(-\log(1-E)\). A cycle with only M? Is it valid? M represents both colors, so yes, valid. A cycle with M and O valid. A cycle with M and E valid. A cycle with M only valid. So invalid only all-O or all-E.
Thus valid cycles EGF = \(-\log(1-O-E-M) + \log(1-O) + \log(1-E)\).
Permutations = exp = \((1-O)(1-E)/(1-O-E-M)\). Yes.

Coefficient derivation:
Let H = 1/(1-O-E-M) = ∑ h_{a,b,c} O^a E^b M^c, h = (a+b+c)!/(a!b!c!).
G = (1-O)(1-E)H = H - O H - E H + OE H.
Coefficient for (o,e,m):
g = h(o,e,m) - h(o-1,e,m) - h(o,e-1,m) + h(o-1,e-1,m).
Multiply by o!e!m!:
Term1: (o+e+m)! = K!.
Term2: o!e!m! * (K-1)!/((o-1)!e!m!) = o (K-1)!.
Term3: e (K-1)!.
Term4: o!e!m! * (K-2)!/((o-1)!(e-1)!m!) = o e (K-2)!.
So count = K! - (o+e)(K-1)! + oe (K-2)!.
Since o+e = K-m.
= (K-2)! [ K(K-1) - (K-m)(K-1) + oe ] = (K-2)! [ m(K-1) + oe ].
This holds for K≥2. For K=1:
g for o=1,e=0,m=0: H coeff (1,0,0)=1, -OH coeff (0,0,0)= -1, others 0 => g=0, count0.
o=0,e=1: count0.
o=0,e=0,m=1: H coeff (0,0,1)=1, no subtraction => g=1, count=1. Good.
For K=0: count1.

Now, how to compute o_count, e_count, m_count from original colors and F components.

Colors: We need color for each index i. Let's define is_odd[i] = (A[i] % 2 == 1). Since A_i rank of s_i. In the orientation, if A_i odd, vertex i is "odd" (source edge forward). If even, "even". The no-mono cycle condition requires each cycle has both odd and even vertices. Yes.

Let's verify with the derived EGF for no forced edges: Count = (N-2)! * R*M. For N=3,R=1,M=2 ->2. This matches feasible b count. Good.

Now, what about the specified B values validation and F construction in terms of colors? The color of a vertex is determined by A_i, independent of B. The forced edges F from B may connect vertices; the path component type is based on colors of vertices in the path. Good.

Let's consider if B_i specified and B_i = -1? We ignore. But what if a specified B_i has v such that q_v = i, and p_v = i? Then F has fixed point i->i. This is a forced cycle length1. If color[i] is either O or E, mono -> invalid. Algorithm will detect cycle length1 mono and output0. Good.

Now, let's think about the condition "In the concatenation of A and B, each integer other than -1 appears at most once." Given A_i distinct, B_i distinct, A_i != B_j. We should still validate:
- A_i distinct? If not, X not permutation likely, but check.
- B_i specified distinct? If two B same, v same? Actually B_i distinct given, but if same, v same and duplicate -> reject.

- Also B_i value must be in 1..2N; given.

Need also validate A ranks imply X permutation. But A_i distinct alone doesn't guarantee; e.g., A=(1,2) invalid. So check.

Let's test a case where A valid but specified B values not in complement? Since B not in A, automatically complement. But if B_i=2v-1 and A contains 2v? Then B is complement. If A contains 2v-1, then B=2v-1 would be in A, rejected. So okay.

Now, let's consider the count of b completions. Our super-vertex count counts permutations of super-vertices, which corresponds to completions of the partial permutation F to a full permutation f with no mono cycles. Each such f corresponds to a unique full b? Recall f = Q ∘ P^{-1}. Given f, Q is determined: q_v = f(p_v). Then B_{q_v} = C_v for all v. Since Q is a permutation, this assigns each t-index a first coordinate v and rank C_v. Thus full b is uniquely determined by f. Conversely, each valid b gives f. So counting f is counting b. Good.

Does each permutation f correspond to exactly one b? Yes, because P fixed and Q = f ∘ P. Since f is a bijection, Q is a bijection. Then for each v, q_v = Q(v) = f(p_v). The t-index q_v gets rank C_v. This defines B_{q_v}=C_v for all v. Since q_v permutation, each B_i is set. Thus b is determined. Good.

Now, what about the specified B constraints: For specified B_i, we fixed q_v=i. In terms of f, f(p_v)=i. Our F is exactly these fixed edges. The remaining f completions determine the unspecified B. Good.


Now, let's think about the original problem's modulo and N up to 3000. Factorials up to N enough. We don't need inverse.

Let's outline final code:

```python
MOD = 998244353

def solve():
    import sys
    input = sys.stdin.readline
    N = int(input())
    A = list(map(int, input().split()))
    B = list(map(int, input().split()))
    
    # validate A
    X = [(a + 1)//2 for a in A]
    seenX = [False]*(N+1)
    p = [0]*(N+1)
    is_odd = [False]*(N+1)
    for i, x in enumerate(X, start=1):
        if x < 1 or x > N or seenX[x]:
            print(0); return
        seenX[x] = True
        p[x] = i
        is_odd[i] = (A[i-1] % 2 == 1)
    
    A_set = set(A)
    
    # specified B validation
    seenB = [False]*(N+1)
    next_edge = [0]*(N+1)
    indeg = [0]*(N+1)
    
    for i, b in enumerate(B, start=1):
        if b == -1:
            continue
        if b < 1 or b > 2*N:
            print(0); return
        if b in A_set:
            print(0); return
        v = (b + 1)//2
        if v < 1 or v > N or seenB[v]:
            print(0); return
        seenB[v] = True
        u = p[v]
        if u == 0:
            print(0); return
        if next_edge[u] != 0 or indeg[i] != 0:
            # conflict, shouldn't happen
            print(0); return
        next_edge[u] = i
        indeg[i] += 1
    
    # components
    visited = [False]*(N+1)
    o = e = m = 0
    K = 0
    
    # path components starting at indeg 0
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        if indeg[i] == 0 and not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while cur != 0 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = next_edge[cur]
            # cur should be 0
            if cur != 0:
                # This would mean path enters a cycle/visited; should not happen.
                # But if it does, treat remaining? We'll just invalid.
                print(0); return
            # process path comp
            has_odd = any(is_odd[v] for v in comp)
            has_even = any(not is_odd[v] for v in comp)
            if has_odd and has_even:
                m += 1
            elif has_odd:
                o += 1
            else:
                e += 1
            K += 1
    
    # cycle components
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = next_edge[cur]
            # comp is a cycle
            has_odd = any(is_odd[v] for v in comp)
            has_even = any(not is_odd[v] for v in comp)
            if not (has_odd and has_even):
                print(0); return
            # valid forced cycle, no super-vertex
    
    # compute answer
    fact = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1, N+1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD
    
    if K == 0:
        ans = 1
    elif K == 1:
        ans = 1 if m == 1 else 0
    else:
        ans = fact[K-2] * ( m * (K-1) + o * e ) % MOD
    print(ans % MOD)
```

Need check the path component loop: For a free vertex i with indeg=0, next=0, comp=[i], cur=0, process. Good.
For a path component start, following next will eventually reach 0 because no path enters cycle. But what if the path component is actually a cycle? Then indeg of all vertices in cycle is 1, so no indeg0 start. So not processed in path loop. Good.
For a vertex with indeg=0 but next points to a vertex in a cycle? We argued impossible due indegree. Let's prove: If u has indeg0 and next[u]=v, and v is in a cycle, then v has indegree from its cycle predecessor and from u, indeg[v]=2. Since indeg array would be 2, but we only increment for forced edges; if u->v forced, indeg[v] includes u. If v in cycle, it also has predecessor in cycle forced, so indeg[v]=2. The input constraints guarantee indeg≤1? Actually F is a partial injection, so indeg≤1. Thus impossible. So cur will be 0.

But wait, what about a path component that starts at indeg0 and ends at a vertex whose next is 0, but along the way some vertex was already visited? Since we start at indeg0 and follow unvisited, no. Good.

Now, the cycle loop: For any unvisited vertex, it must be in a cycle. We follow next until visited. Since it's a cycle, we will return to i and stop. The comp list will contain the cycle in order starting at i. But note: If we start at i in a cycle, we mark i, follow next. Eventually cur becomes i, which is visited, loop stops. comp contains all cycle vertices. Good.

Now, what about a forced cycle of length1 (fixed point): indeg=1, no path loop, cycle loop comp=[i], has_odd maybe true, has_even false -> invalid. Good.

Now, let's test with sample 1 step by step:
N=3, A [2,3,6], X [1,2,3], p[1]=1,p[2]=2,p[3]=3, is_odd [False,True,False].
B [-1,1,-1], b=1 not in A, v=1, seenB[1], u=p[1]=1, next[1]=2, indeg[2]=1.
Path loop:
i=1 indeg0 -> comp [1,2], cur next[2]=0, has_odd True (2), has_even True (1) -> m=1,K=1.
i=2 indeg1 skip.

Now, let's think about the derivation of the EGF for super-vertices with M type in terms of labeled combinatorial classes. The variables O,E,M are EGF variables; the count formula uses factorial denominators. We derived count = o!e!m! * coeff. Since super-vertices are labeled and types are fixed, the total number of permutations of these specific labeled items is given by that. Good.

Now, let's ensure we don't need to multiply by the number of ways to choose which path component is which type. The types are determined by the components; the EGF coefficient for given counts o,e,m counts permutations of any labeled items with those types. Since our super-vertices are already labeled, the count formula is directly the number of permutations. No additional binomial factors. Let's verify with no F: K=N, o=R,e=M,m=0. Formula (N-2)! R M. We also derived R!M! * binom(N-2,R-1) = R*M*(N-2)!. They match. Good.

Now, let's think about if a forced path component type M should be considered as a single labeled item with type M. The EGF coefficient for m M items counts permutations of m distinct M-labeled items. Our m_count is the number of such components, each distinct. Good.

The forced cycle is a fixed cycle of f; it is already determined by F. There is no choice in its internal order. It doesn't interact with other cycles. So factor1. Good.

Now, let's write final code.

```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return
    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1+N]
    B = data[1+N:1+2*N]
    ...
```

Need ensure B length N. Good.

Let's implement validation.

Potential issue: `A_set = set(A)` but if A invalid X, we still might use A_set? We check X first, then A_set. If invalid, return.

Let's code:

```
    X = [(a+1)//2 for a in A]
    seenX = [False]*(N+1)
    p = [0]*(N+1)
    is_odd = [False]*(N+1)
    for idx, x in enumerate(X, 1):
        if x < 1 or x > N or seenX[x]:
            print(0); return
        seenX[x] = True
        p[x] = idx
        is_odd[idx] = (A[idx-1] & 1)
```

Note `is_odd[idx] = bool(A[idx-1] & 1)`.

Then B validation:

```
    A_set = set(A)
    seenB = [False]*(N+1)
    nxt = [0]*(N+1)
    indeg = [0]*(N+1)
    for i,b in enumerate(B,1):
        if b == -1:
            continue
        if b < 1 or b > 2*N:
            print(0); return
        if b in A_set:
            print(0); return
        v = (b+1)//2
        if v < 1 or v > N or seenB[v]:
            print(0); return
        seenB[v] = True
        u = p[v]
        if u == 0:
            print(0); return
        if nxt[u] != 0 or indeg[i] != 0:
            print(0); return
        nxt[u] = i
        indeg[i] = 1
```

Use indeg[i] = 1 instead of +=1 since check.

Then components:

```
    visited = [False]*(N+1)
    o = e = m = 0
    K = 0

    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if indeg[i] == 0 and not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while cur != 0 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = nxt[cur]
            if cur != 0:
                print(0); return
            has_odd = False
            has_even = False
            for v in comp:
                if is_odd[v]:
                    has_odd = True
                else:
                    has_even = True
            if has_odd and has_even:
                m += 1
            elif has_odd:
                o += 1
            else:
                e += 1
            K += 1
```

Wait: What if a free vertex has indeg0 and next=0, comp length1. Good.

But what about a vertex with indeg0 that is the start of a path, but the path end's next is 0. We process. Good.

Now, after path loop, unvisited cycles:

```
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        if not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = nxt[cur]
            has_odd = False
            has_even = False
            for v in comp:
                if is_odd[v]:
                    has_odd = True
                else:
                    has_even = True
            if not (has_odd and has_even):
                print(0); return
```

No K increment for cycles.

Then factorial and answer.

One subtlety: In the path loop, if a vertex has indeg0 but is part of a cycle? impossible. But what about a vertex with indeg0 and next=0 that is unvisited; comp length1. Good.

Another subtlety: If a path component has length >1 and contains both colors, we count m. If it contains only even, e. If only odd, o. Good.

Now, let's think about if a forced path component of length >1 has both colors but the start and end colors? Doesn't matter.

Now, let's consider if a forced path component type M and K=1, ans1. The formula handles K==1.

Now, let's implement factorial:

```
    fact = [1]*(N+1)
    for i in range(1,N+1):
        fact[i] = fact[i-1]*i % MOD

    if K == 0:
        ans = 1
    elif K == 1:
        ans = 1 if m == 1 else 0
    else:
        ans = fact[K-2] * (m * (K-1) + o * e) % MOD
    print(ans)
```

Let's test sample 2 with this code mentally gave sample. Good.

Now, let's think about if K=1 and m=1 but there are forced cycles? Example forced cycle mixed plus one M path. ans1. Good.

Now, let's consider if K=1 and m=0 but o/e=0? impossible because K=1 means one path component, so either o/e/m. If m=0, ans0. Good.

Now, let's think about if K=0 and there are no path components but there are forced cycles all mixed. ans1. If there are no forced cycles and no path components? N=0 only. Good.

Now, let's consider if the partial injection F has a path component that is type M but the path length is 1? impossible. But if it did, m would be false because a single vertex cannot have both colors. Good.

Now, let's think about the validation of B specified if it creates an edge u->i where u and i are the same? That's a fixed point. We don't reject in B validation; component cycle invalid. Good.

=== ANSWER ===
```python
import sys

MOD = 998244353

def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.read().split()))
    if not data:
        return

    N = data[0]
    A = data[1:1 + N]
    B = data[1 + N:1 + 2 * N]

    # Validate A and compute p[v] = index i with X_i = v
    X = [(a + 1) // 2 for a in A]
    seen_x = [False] * (N + 1)
    p = [0] * (N + 1)
    is_odd = [False] * (N + 1)

    for i, x in enumerate(X, start=1):
        if x < 1 or x > N or seen_x[x]:
            print(0)
            return
        seen_x[x] = True
        p[x] = i
        is_odd[i] = (A[i - 1] & 1) == 1

    A_set = set(A)

    # Build the partial permutation forced by specified B
    nxt = [0] * (N + 1)
    indeg = [0] * (N + 1)
    seen_b = [False] * (N + 1)

    for i, b in enumerate(B, start=1):
        if b == -1:
            continue

        if b < 1 or b > 2 * N:
            print(0)
            return
        if b in A_set:
            print(0)
            return

        v = (b + 1) // 2
        if v < 1 or v > N or seen_b[v]:
            print(0)
            return
        seen_b[v] = True

        u = p[v]
        if u == 0:
            print(0)
            return
        if nxt[u] != 0 or indeg[i] != 0:
            print(0)
            return

        nxt[u] = i
        indeg[i] = 1

    visited = [False] * (N + 1)

    # Counts of contracted path-components by type
    odd_cnt = 0
    even_cnt = 0
    mixed_cnt = 0
    K = 0  # number of path-components (including isolated vertices)

    # Path components: start from indegree 0
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if indeg[i] == 0 and not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while cur != 0 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = nxt[cur]

            if cur != 0:
                print(0)
                return

            has_odd = False
            has_even = False
            for v in comp:
                if is_odd[v]:
                    has_odd = True
                else:
                    has_even = True

            if has_odd and has_even:
                mixed_cnt += 1
            elif has_odd:
                odd_cnt += 1
            else:
                even_cnt += 1

            K += 1

    # Remaining components are forced cycles
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        if not visited[i]:
            comp = []
            cur = i
            while cur != 0 and not visited[cur]:
                visited[cur] = True
                comp.append(cur)
                cur = nxt[cur]

            if cur == 0:
                print(0)
                return

            has_odd = False
            has_even = False
            for v in comp:
                if is_odd[v]:
                    has_odd = True
                else:
                    has_even = True

            # A forced cycle must contain both colors
            if not (has_odd and has_even):
                print(0)
                return

    fact = [1] * (N + 1)
    for i in range(1, N + 1):
        fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD

    if K == 0:
        ans = 1
    elif K == 1:
        ans = 1 if mixed_cnt == 1 else 0
    else:
        ans = fact[K - 2] * (mixed_cnt * (K - 1) + odd_cnt * even_cnt) % MOD

    print(ans)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```
